746. MARGARET SHEEN (16), and EDWARD CLARKE (26) , Unlawfully attempting to steal 1 florin, the money of Edward Lee.
MR. COOPER conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN LANGFORD . I live in Trafalgar-road, Greenwich—on 8th April, a little after 4 o'clock, I saw the prisoners with another men and woman drinking in the Lord Nelson public-house, Greenwich; soon afterwards Sheen came into my shop—in consequence of something I had heard I followed her out, first to the Victoria and then to the Lord Nelson, where they joined, and on their leaving there I followed them as far as the College—I there pointed them out to Bidgland—I saw no more of them.
Cross-examined by MR. DICKIE. Q. What became of the other two? A. I don't know; they were all four together when I first Saw them—Ridgman is not here; he is too ill to attend.
JOHN LUCAS . I keep a tobacconist's shop on Blackheath-hill—about 8 o'clock on the evening of 8th April Sheen came, for two cigars, and tendered a half-sovereign in payment—I tested it by biting it—upon that she called in Clarke, and said, "Ned, this man has bent your half-sovereign"—I said, "If I have, and it is a good one, I will give you change"—the man made a bit of a bother—he said, "Come up to the jeweller's and have it tested"—we went to a jeweller's, and it was found to be a good one—we then returned to the shop, and I gave 9 s. 9 d. change—the two cigars came to 3 d.—Clarke refused to take the cigars and his 9 s. 9 d., and wanted another half-sovereign instead of the one I had bent—I refused to give him another one, and eventually he took the change and the cigars, and they left.
EDWIN BOTTRELL . I keep the Fisherman's Arms, Greenwich—on the night of 8th April, about half past 9, the two prisoners and another woman came, and called for a pint of ale; my brother served them, and gave them change for a half-sovereign—Sheen asked for two bottles of ginger-beer to take away with her, and gave a half-sovereign in payment—my brother gave her two half-crowns and 4 s. change—as soon as she had got the change in her hand she said she wanted three bottles—I said I did not keep them—she then wanted her half-sovereign back, and she put 8 s. on the counter instead of 9 s.—I distinctly saw the other shilling in her hand; my brother asked her for the other shilling—she said, "That is all you gave me"—my brother caught hold of her hand, and she dropped it on the onside of the counter—as soon as Sheen bad got the change, Clarke and the other woman left—I went and brought Clarke back, and charged him with being connected
with her—he denied knowing anything of her till, we got to the door, and then he said, "Give my girl the half-sovereign back."
JEREMIAH HENESST ( Policeman, R 192). I was called to Mr. Lucas's, and found the prisoners there, disputing about a half-sovereign—I saw Sheen take up the change, 9 s. 9 d.—Clarke said, "What the b—y h—i do you think we stole it; what me and my sister had to work hard for all the, week"—from there I saw the two prisoners go into the George and Dragon with another woman, and afterwards to the Coach and Horses, and the Duke of York; they then all went into the Banshee beer-shop—I was afterwards called to the Fisherman's Arms, where I received the prisoners in charge from Mr. Bottrell for attempting to steal a shilling.
Cross-examined. Q. What became of the other female? A. She was taken into custody, but was discharged.
SHEEN — GUILTY .— Confined One Month.
CLARKE — GUILTY .*— Confined Six Months.

755. JOHN CROCKETT (24) , Unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MBSSBS. POLAND and ORRIDGE conducted the Prosecution, MR. BRST defended.
ANNIE PRICK . I live with my father, John Price, who keeps the Wellington Arms, Waterloo-road—on 25th April, between 4 and 5, the prisoner came with four women and asked for a pot of ale—he paid with a half-crown—I looked at it and told him it was bad—he said, "I will give you another," and he gave me half a crown, which I saw was likewise bad—I then sent for a constable—one of the women then snatched the money out of the prisoner's left hand, and they all four ran away—the prisoner bad been drinking slightly.
Cross-examined by MR. BEST. Q. He was drunk was he not? A. I should not say he was drunk—he was leaning on the counter—he did not say anything.
MR. ORRIDGE. Q. To whom did you give the shilling and half-crown? A. To the constable.
JOHN COLLINS ( Policeman, K 184). I was sent for to the Wellington Arms, and prisoner was given into custody—the last witness gave me a shilling and half-crown ( produced)—I found this bad florin in prisoner's left hand—at the station I searched him, and found half a crown and three shillings good money in his trousers pocket—I told him the half-crown was a bad one; he made no reply—he had the appearance of having been drinking—he knew what he was about.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he give his correct name and address? A. Yes.
WILLUM WEBSTIR . The coins produced are counterfeit.
GUILTY .— Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18630511-756]
756. EMMA CLARK (25), was indicted for a like offence. MESSRS. POLAND and ORRIDGE conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZA ARNOLD . I am a clerk in the telegraph office, 68, London-road—on 24th April, the prisoner came to my office at about 11 in the morning, with a message to be forwarded, but it was retained for non-delivery—the person to whom it was directed was not found—she paid half a crown, the message was 6 d.—I gave her the 2 s. and put the half-crown in the till—she came again about half-past 2 on the same day, and then gave me a message to send—I did not forward that—she paid with a half a crown—I sent it to be changed next door by McDonald—he brought it back and I then told the prisoner it was bad—she said she should not think of passing a bad one—I examined the till while she was there, and found three more bad half-crowns—I bad other silver in the till, but the bad half-crowns were all lying at the top—I cannot tell exactly at what time I received those without looking at the messages—there was one at 1.25, another at 1.35, and the prisoner came first about 11—those were not the only messages I took that day, but the only ones paid by half-crowns—I told the prisoner the half crown was bad, and that she had been before that morning—she denied it—I gave the half-crowns to the constable—I am quite sure she had been at the office—the two messages produced, I received between 11 and the prisoner coming—the other two ( produced) I took from prisoner—they all four appear to be written by the same person.
WILLIAM MCDONALD . I am a messenger in the telegraph office, London road—on 24th April, I received from last witness a half-crown which I took
next door to get changed—it was tried in my presence and returned to me—I took it back to Miss Arnold.
DAVID HATTERSLEY ( Policeman, P 292). I took the prisoner into custody on 24th April—Miss Arnold gave me a half-crown which I produce—the prisoner was searched, and two sixpences good money were found upon her.
WILLIAM WERSITER The half-crowns produced are all bad.
Prisoner's Defence. I went out about quarter past 2 and I met a gentle-man. He told me if I would take a message to the telegraph office ha would give me a shilling. I took the Message, and when I gave in the piece of paper I gave half a crown; this young person put it down where there was some other money, and then she took it up and said it was bad; she accused me of going there at 11. I never left my home until a quarter-past 2, and I met the gentleman at half-past 2. He gave ma a half crown, and said I was to bring back 2 s. and pay 6 d. for the message; I had no idea it was bad.
GUILTY — Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18630511-757]
757. MARIA FROST (37), and ELIZABETH MASON (42) , Feloniously administering to Frederick Wyatt, a certain stupifying thing; to enable them to steal his purse and money.
MR. DICKIE conducted The Prosecution.
FREDERICK WYATT . I am living now at Liverpool—I was, on the 24th April, staying at 59, Victoria-terrace, Union-road, Southwalk—on that day I met a woman in Newington-causeway, and went with her to 17, Revel's-row, but she did not stop—I saw prisoners there with a third wonsan, who got away—they asked me what I would give them to drink, and I gave them a shilling twice to get rum, of which I drank about a quarter of a glass—when I had been there about three-quarters of an hour, the one that got away forced me on to a bed and put her hand to my face; one was on one aide and one on another, and Mason held my feet—I was completely senseless, though I could see everything they were doing—I am quite confident the two prisoners were in the room—there was 8 l. in gold, and about 11 s. in silver in my pockets—Frost took the money out of my right side pocket—they knew the money I had with me—the party I went with told ma to take care what money I had got, and I counted the whole of my money which they saw—previously to forcing me on the bed, they used a white handkerchief to me that had chloroform, of which I know the smell quite well—I was paralysed—I remained helpless after that—it was between 5 and 6 before I came to myself—I went to the police-station at half-past 6.
Cross-examined by MR. LILLEY. Q. Where do you live? A. I arrived. in Liverpool on 3d February, from Smyrna, and I had then been stopping at 59, Victoria-terrace, but I am no stranger in London—I have been in the Ottoman Empire the last three years—! was only visiting them—my business is connected with railways—I am here under a doctor's certificate for the benefit of my health—I was in the fourth division of the London police before I went to Asia Minor—I left that to go to the South Eastern Railway—I left of my own accord—I resigned—I remained with the South Eastern Railway about eighteen months—I then left to better myself, of my own accord—I have merely come home from Asia Minor on account of the fever—that is the only cause—I have had money offered to me to forego this prosecution by five or six people, but I have not offered to take any—I have not offered to take 3 l —I do not know Sally Brown or Sally Bishop—I did not offer to her to take 3 l.—I did not draw up a paper, which I proposed to send to the Magistrate in case I received a certain sum, saying I would not proceed with the prosecution—there was a paper of that
sort handed to me, but it was not my conditions at all—I saw that paper, but I did not accept of it—I did not show it to Hussey, the pot man—I have been offered money, but I would not take it, on certain conditions—I do not think it was likely I should say, I would go right away if I got a certain sum of money; I was bound in 40 l. to appear—I never made that offer in the presence of' Hussey—I know a woman who goes by the name of Mrs. White—I did not say to her, I would accept 3 l. to forego this prosecution—I had been to the house in question before—I went there between 3 and 4—I gave Frost 2 s. to get drink twice, giving her 1 s. on each occasion—no one else went for drink—there were three women in my company when the robbery occurred—there was one before, but she did not stop long, only about five minutes—I did not remain with her half an hour; she did not give me her company—she merely came to the door, and went inside, and spoke to me, and went away—I found the prisoners there when I went, and another woman came about ten minutes after I arrived there—she did not come at my request—she remained about a quarter of an hour—I bad seen her face before, bat do not know her name; she was the one who contrived with the two prisoners to rob me—it was nearly six when I left—I had not been into any public-house before I went—I had taken a glass of half-and-half for dinner at my sister's—I had nothing in addition—it is some years ago since I acquired my experience of chloroform; I have smelt it—I have never inhaled it; nor undergone any operation which rendered it necessary—I knew Dr. McCree some years ago, and have taken out a bottle, and smelt It—I should think the day in question was the second time I have been in this house since I have been in this country—I went out to Asia Minor in October, 1860—I saw five persons during the time I was in this house.
MR. DICKIE. Q. Is there any pretence for saying you were tipsy when in this house? A. None whatever; the officer will prove I was quite sober.
WILLIAM CUMMING ( Policeman, M 196). On 24th April I went to the house of prisoner Frost, at 17, Revel's-row, at 7 o'clock—I did now see her then—at 8 o'clock I saw her there with Mason—I told her I wanted her for robbing a man of a quantity of money—I commenced searching the place, and she said, "It is no use, you won't find it"—I said, "Find what?"—she replied, "Nothing"—I had another constable, I told him to take Mason, and she ran up stairs.
LOT SAWYER ( Policeman, M 175). I went with the last witness to take the prisoners into custody on the 24th April—I saw them both—Mason ran up stairs, and I followed her, and I saw her breaking the string of a new pair of foots—I asked what she had got, and she said she had bought them the day before in Suffolk-street—I told her I should take her into custody for being concerned in robbing Mr. Wyatt—I took her to the station, and afterwards went to Mr. Durrant's with the other constable, and made inquiries about some boots—I afterwards made inquiries at the Skinner's Arms, and I saw the witness, Hussey.
COURT. Q. Did you see the prosecutor before yon went? A. I did between 7 and 8—he was the worse for drink; he had been drinking, by his appearance—I did go near enough to smell if it was anything except drink.
WILLIAM DURRANIT . I am a shoemaker, of 21, Great Suffolk-street, Borough—on 24th April, Frost bought a pair of boots of me—I have known her for several years; she has regularly dealt with us—it was in the afternoon she had the boots—I cannot say exactly the time—in the
evening she came with somebody else, and bought a pair of boot for 3 s. 9 d.,—she gave me 4 s., and I gave her 3 d. back—these ( produced) are the boots.
WILLIAM HUSSEY . I am barman at the Skinner's Arms public-house, Great Suffolk-street, Borough—I remember Frost coming in on the 24th April, about 5 in the afternoon—she gave me a sovereign and a half, and asked me to put it on the shelf; and mind it for her—I did not see the officer, it was my master.
Cross-examined. Q. Hare you seen the prosecutor since this matter? A. Yes—on one occasion he showed me a paper that he had written out—he said he intended to present it to the Magistrate, wishing to withdraw the case—on several occasions he has asked parties round to make up a sum of money to withdraw from the case, as he wished to have nothing at all to do with it, and he would go away, and keep away altogether until such time as it was over—I, have heard him offers to accept money for the purpose of going away on several occasions—it is a very common case that we have money left with us by various people to take care of—Frost has before deposited money with my master, but not with me.
MR. DICKIE. Q. Have you not offered to pawn your watch to get Frost out trouble, and give the money to the prosecutor? A. Never—I said yesterday if it was possible to get Counsel to plead for both, as I had not the means in my possession, then I would pawn my watch to get the money, if I thought the parties would pay me back—I have known Frost three years as coming to our house—the prosecutor told me that he wrote that statement.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18630511-758]
758. EDWIN ROGERS (24) , Stealing 6 window-sashes, and other property, the goods of John Thomas Peacock, fixed to a building.
MIL BULKY conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM WINTER . I am a cheesemonger, living at Lower-road, Islington—I became possessed of the house No. 4, Corthorpe-terrace, Bermondsey-new-road, from the Governors of St. Thomas's Hospital, and Mr. Booth by a lease for twenty-one years, and I occupied under that lease—when it was granted I laid out a sum of money in fitting up the place for the business of a cheesemonger—I put in a new counter, new gas-fittings, and new mahogany sashes, plate-glass, new shelves, and deal lining—the whole cost me 95 l ——there was not a leaden cistern in the yard—on 13th August, I sold the good—will and fixtures, and assigned the lease to the prisoner—the assignment it endorsed on the back of the lease ( produced)—I have seen the prisoner there, carrying on business under that assignment.
THOMAS ABBOTT . I am assistant messenger in the Court of Bankruptcy—I produce the proceedings in the bankruptcy of the prisoner, and of Anthony Voght—the prisoner's petition is dated 26th July, 1862, and the adjudication is 29th July, 1862—the appointment of the prosecutor as creditor's assignee is 19th of August, 1862—those proceedings are still pending—Anthony Voght filed two petitions, one on 12th March, 1863, and the second, the 14th March.
BENJAMIN MARSLAND . I am articled clerk to Mr. Sheene, of Harp-lane, Lower-street, solicitor—I have been collector of the rent for No. 4, Corthorpe-terrace, Bermondsey-new-road, for the superior landlord under the lease—I had occasion to go constantly to the shop during the latter part of last year—the prisoner was present—I was there in the middle of February—the shop was then quite complete with counter and lining, and gas-fittings
—there was a pair of scales and set of weights and marble-blocks, and all the fittings of a cheesemonger—I called one Saturday evening in February, I think the 14th, about the rent—the prisoner could not pay, and he got into a terrible pet, swore very badly, and said be would cut every b—y thing to pieces—on the Monday morning I went round to levy a distress, and the place was shut up.
JOHN THOMAS PEACOCK . I am an egg merchant, carrying on business in Berwick-street, Oxford-street, and I was appointed creditors' assignee, under the prisoner's bankruptcy—I endeavoured to obtain possession of property in his order and disposition—objection was made to my taking this lease—I cannot say precisely what was said—as to the fixtures you will see what was seized by the bill of sale—they were claimed by the prisoner's father—the prisoner did not say anything to me about it—it is in evidence in the Court of Bankruptcy—I took proceedings against Anthony Voght—the prisoner was present at the trial, which took place, I believe, on the 15th of December last year—I recovered a verdict—I endeavoured after the action to get possession of this place and the fixtures at the latter part of April—I then found the premises completely gutted—no one was in the place, and I effected an entrance, breaking in—I found a cistern had been removed—I did not notice whether the ballcock was gone the windowsashes and gas fittings were taken away.
GEORGE CARTER I am a furniture dealer, living at Pitt-street, old Kent-road—I was employed by the prisoner some time in February to go to 4, Cortbope-terrace, Bermondsey-new-road, and I went—we assisted in moving away the fittings of the shop, the counter, weights, gas fittings, and sashes with plate-glass—I did not take what is called the match boarding—I did not notice whether it had been taken down—I took the things away in a barrow to my place, and they were from there on the same day—on first removing the things some were taken out at the front way, and some the back way, over a wall, and through another house, and then by a back street—they were removed by me to Mr. Hill's—he made the bargain with the prisoner—I had nothing to do with that.
JOHN HILL . I am a dealer in fixtures of New-cut. Lambeth—in February last the prisoner came with some one to me, and brought some scales, gas-fittings, a few match-board deals, a butter-block, and two mahogany sashes, fitted with plate-glass—I think I gave him 5 l. or 6 l. for the first lot—I produce a receipt for 4 l. 10 s —the window-sashes came at two different times—I think I gave him about 11 l. altogether.
FREDERICK INDERMAUR . I am an auctioneer and value, carrying on business in Union-street, Southwark—on 16th April I examined the promises 4, Cortherpe-terrace—I went to distrain, but could not get in—they were in a very dilapidated state—the counter was gone—the lining-board had been removed—some portion was left on, and others had been wrenched off—I did not notice any cistern, or the place where there should have been one.
THE RECORDER was Of opinion that the indictment for felony could not be supported, at there was lawful possession, and no determination, of the tenancy, and directed a verdiet of
NOT GUILTY .

759. EDWIN ROGERS was again indicted for unlawfully pulling down from a freehold house certain fixtures.
WILLAM WINTER . ( The evidence of ibis witness in the former case was read over). I have seen the premises since April—there was nothing in
them at all—the damage had bean done inside—I saw the damage done by the water, in consequence of the removal of the cistern—it was cut off from the pipe—I did not see any water—there was a ball-cock when I took the shop.
BENJAMIN MARSLAND . ( The evidence of this witness in the former case was read over). On the 15th April, when I got possession of the place, the cistern had been removed, and the water had flooded the place ankle-deep,—there was nothing in the shop, the lining had been torn down recklessly—it was a perfect wreck, and the damage would be a considerable amount—the water poured in when turned on from the main.
GEORGE CARTER . ( The former evidence of this witness was read over.) I went to the premises more than once—I could not take all the things in one barrow—there was nothing taken down while I was there-three out of the four panes of glass in the frame were sound, one was cracked—I took down the gas-fittings and counter, but not the match boarding—as regards what I did I had no trouble—I helped to take the water cistern down—the pipe was bent up to prevent the water flowing.
THOMAS ABBOTT . ( The evidence of this witness in the former case was read over)
FREDERICK INDERMAUR . ( The former evidence of this witness was read over I did not see any water in the premises—the boarding was removed in quite the reverse of workman-like manner—it was torn out—it was pulled down not merely carelessly, but wantonly—I should think the whole damage would be 40 l. or 50 l.
Prisoner's Defence. I thought I had a right to take the things; I paid 500 l. for the shop, and I lost about 600 l.; I was awfully taken in. If I have done wrong I am willing to work; hard to pay it back.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Serjeant.
[trialAccount t18630511-760]
760. JAMES FREDERICKS (27) , Burglary in the dwelling-house of Samuel Upham Whittel, and stealing 1 pair of trousers, and other articles, the property of Henry Madge.
MR. HORRY conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY MADGE . I am a salesman at Messrs. Perks, Shad Thames, and live at 18, Trinity-square, in the house of Mr. Whittel, in the parish of St. Mary, Newington—on the morning of 10th April I left my home as usual, and, I believe, I left in the chest of drawers in my room, four waist-coats, two shirts, and other articles—t afterwards saw a portion of them at, the station-house—I returned home at half-past I on the morning of the 11th, and found my room in a general state of confusion—it is on the first-floor back a bedroom—the drawers were emptied, and the clothes scattered all about the room—I did not examine sufficiently to miss any articles at that time—I called the servant—I Afterwards saw the prisoner in custody at the police-station, and these articles ( produced), which are mine—the prisoner had some of the things on—I believe they were taken from him at the police-station.
Prisoner. Did yon not say when yon came to the station that you believed those were the clothes, and that if they were they would be marked? A. Yes—I examined the shirt—I did not notice any marks, but I did not examine it thoroughly—I did not know at the time where they were marked—I asked to take the shirt home with me to compare it with some that I had—I was not half an hour away, I believe—I will swear I brought back
the same shirt—I do not know whether the flannel was marked—I said I should know the black trousers by a kind of mark at the bottom—I swear that all these articles are mine—I have not said that I held the person as he was coming out, or that I saw his face—I saw you passing hastily—I could not swear to you—I said there was stitching at the bottom of the trousers of a particular kind before I looked at them—the policeman examined them and found the stitching, and upon that I swore to them.
CHARLOTTE BEECHING . I am servant to Mr. Whittel, of 18, Trinity-square—on 10th April, we went to bed at twenty minutes to 11—I made the house secure, in the usual way; we are rather careless—the front kitchen window was closed; I cannot say it was fastened—the window-shutters were to, and a desk and work-box were against the shutters—I went into Mr. Madge's room as I went up stairs, and it was perfectly straight, as usual—at about 2 o'clock—I was called up—I went down stairs, found the kitchen shutters were open, and the work-box and desk on the floor, and the contents turned out—there was a mark on the area railings, as if some one had put their foot over and come down the wall; there are no steps or gate to the area—if a person got into the area, he had nothing to do but throw up the window to get in—I afterwards went into the front parlour—I looked under the table, and there was a man there—there was light enough for me to perceive that he was in his shirt sleeves, and had no shoes on—he rushed past me to the front door, which was open, and rushed out—I do not know who had opened the front door.
Prisoner. Q. What time was it when you were awoke? A. As near as I can guess, about a quarter to 2—about twenty minute* elapsed from my first being awaked to the person leaving the house—I was up stairs, looking over the place.
JOHN POMERT ( Policeman, M 130). On the morning of the 11th April, I was on duty in Trinity-square—I heard a cry of "Police," and went to No. 18, and first went into the front kitchen—in the parlour I found four waistcoats and two shirts, which had been conveyed there from the bedroom—those were afterwards claimed by Mr. Madge—I went to Mr. Madge's room, and found a coat, two dirty shirts, and a pair of blucher boots there—Mr. Madge, at the police-station, first told me the articles found were his; then he afterwards said, "Let me hare them and put them on"—they fitted him pretty well—I had ordered the prisoner to take them off—in the pocket of the prisoner's clothes I found a seaman's discharge, and some matches—when I took the prisoner to the station-house, he had on some of the prosecutor's' clothes.
Prisoner. Q. Did you accompany Mr. Madge to the station-house? A. I did—I said, "Here he is"—from the description Mr. Madge gave to me, I told him to come to the station-house, and most probably he would see the man—the description was, a person going away without shoes and stockings, in his shirt sleeves, and without a hat—you were ordered to take off the clothes, and he tried to find a name on them, but without success—he then asked for the shirt to take home and compare with the others—the shirt did not go out of my sight—the mark on the shirt I did look for, but it was almost invisible—there was a gas light there—Mr. Madge said he had every reason to believe the articles were his.
DAVID DAVIS ( Policeman, A 473). At half-past 2, on the 11th April, I heard a cry of "Stop thief," and saw a person running down New Kent road, from St. George's-road and Trinity-square—it is some distance from Trinity-square—I sprung my rattle, and ran across the road, and caught
the prisoner, who was in his shirt sleeves, and without a hat or boots—he said, "For God's sake, don't stop me, policeman; I am a seaman, and have been robbed"—I hit him with my rattle, and there is some of his blood on the shirt—I took him to the station-house.
WILLIAM DUFFEL ( Policeman, M 151). At half-past 2, on 11th April, I saw the prisoner jump over the sash window of No. 43, St. George's-road—I followed him, and never lost sight of him until he was secured and taken back.
The prisoner called
CHARLOTTE MAYNARD . I heard a noise in my bedroom at about a quarter-past 2, on 11th April—I know it Was that time, because I looked at the clock when the officer brought back the prisoner to my house.
Prisoner's Defence. I went to the music-hall with a couple of females, and after I left I suddenly became insensible, and I remember no more until I found myself in the room of the last witness. She asked, "Who is there?" I said, "I am a sailor, who has been robbed." I tried to get out; the door was locked, and I jumped out of window. The trousers and waistcoat I claim as my own property.
GUILTY .
The prisoner was further charged with having been formerly convicted of felony, on 31st March, at Kingston-upon-Thames; to which he
PLEADED GUILTY.**— Six Years' Penal Servitude.
[trialAccount t18630511-761]
761. JOHN BROWN (27), and CHARLES KEMBLE (22), Stealing 3 rings, value 7 l., the property of John Trafalgar Black, in his dwelling-house.
MR. PALMER conducted the Prosecution.
MARIAN BLACK. I am the wife of John Trafalgar Black, a tutor, of 4, Hamilton-place, Brixton-road—on 27th April, the prisoners came—my servant let them into the dining-room, and then into the drawing-room—I followed them, and asked them what apartment they wanted—they said a dewing-room floor and two bedrooms—they told me they were professional singers, engaged at different houses, and that they kept a "trap"—I showed them the drawing-room floor; there was no property of mine them—the servant then took them to this second-floor—my bedroom is on that floor, Over the drawing-room—I heard some one overhead, in my room—the servant then brought them down—they said there was another professional singer, and wished to know what other bedroom I could let them have—knowing that they had been in my room, I suspected them, and directed my servant to show her bedroom, to give me time—they went up stairs again, and I ran up to my bedroom, and missed three rings, which I had left on the looking-glass, worth 6 l. or 7 l.—I then came down, and waited at the drawing-room door until the prisoners came down—I went into the ball before them—they said the apartment would suit them very well—I said, "I wish to speak to you; step into the dining-room for a minute,"—they said their time was up, and they must go—I said, "I cannot allow you to go until you have given me the three rings which you have taken from my bedroom"—I put the chain on the front door—they were then in the hall, coming towards the door—I stood before them, and would not let them pass—they said, "Oh, dear no, madam; the very idea of such a thing! we are too respectable; we have never been guilty of such a thing"—I said, "You have them"—there was a life-preserver hanging on the folding-door between the front and back room; Kemble took it off the door—I said, "You had better give me up the rings; what do you intend doing f you will be transported; if you have no fear of man, have you no fear of God? if yon give me up the rings, I will release you"—there was a friend of nine down Stairs—I called him up—as he was coming up, the prisoners said, "If you
look round the room, you will find your property"—I had seen Brown put something on the sofa, and I said, "I saw where you put them"—he then took the three rings between his thumb and finger, brought them to me, and said, "Now you have got your property, you will allow us to go"—I said, "No"—I thought I saw the life-preserver in one of the prisoners' pocket—it had been lying on the table when my friend came up, and one of them took it off—they refused to give up the rings until the police were at the door.
Cross-examined by MR. DICKIE. ( For Brown) Q. Had you sent for a policeman? A. Yes, I had sent my servant, and heard her putting the key in the door—the moment they heard footsteps, Brown dropped the rings—the door was opened by my friend, and there were the servant and two policemen.
Cross-examined by MR. SMITH. ( For Kamble). Q. When they came to the house, I suppose you thought them respectable? A. I had my doubts—I let my servant show them over the premises instead of going with them—there was no property in the bed-room which the servant showed them—her room is on the third floor, and mine is on the second floor—Kemble was the one that took the life-preserver—there was no attempt to strike me—I said if they gave up the rings I would let them go, because I thought it would give me time to send for a policeman—perhape when I made that promise I had the intention of breaking it—I intended to see whether they had robbed me of my property—when you have thieves in the house you do the best you can.
Dickie. Q. Did yon ever say anything about the life-preserver before today? A. I did to the policeman—I was not asked about it before the Magistrate—it was taken off the door in my presence—it was not attempted to be used—I am certain I saw the rings when last in my room—I had taken them off to wash my hands.
MR. PALMER. Q. Had you any other motive in making the promise but the laudable strategy of gaining time? A. No.
WILLIAM LAKE ( Policeman, P 264). I was called, and look the prisoners at Hamilton-place—they made no statement—they were given in charge for stealing the rings—they gave a false address—there was another constable.
Kemble received a good character. — GUILTY .— Four Years' Penal Servitude each.
There were several other similar indictments against the prisoners.

763. JOHN GARRATT (41) , Unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MESSRS. POLAND conducted the Prosecution.
EMMA KINGSFORD . I am waitress at a coffee-house, 10, Duke-street, Southwark—on the morning of the 8th April, about half-past 1, the prisoner came in and had some provisions to the amount of 7 1/2 d.—he gave me a half-crown, and I gave him Is. 10 1/2 d. change—I put the half-crown in my pocket—I had no other there—the prisoner went away, and in about twenty minutes he came in again, ordered similar provisions, and gave me another half-crown; I looked at it, saw it was bad, and told him so—he said it was a good one—I sent for my master, and took the other one out of my pocket and found that was also bad—I gave them both to my master—the prisoner was given into custody—he said he did not know the money was bad.
GEORGE WALTER SMITH , I keep the coffee-house, 10, Duke-street—on 8th April the last witness gave me two bad half-crowns; I sent for a constable, and gave the prisoner in charge, with the half-crowns.
ROBERT PIERCEY ( Policeman, M 299). I took the prisoner—I produce two half-crowns, which I got from Mr. Smith—I found 3 1/2 d. in coppers on the prisoner—I asked him his address, and he refused to tell me.
WILLIAM WEBSTER . These half-crowns are both bad.
GUILTY .— Confined Four Months.

765. JAMES FRENCH (42) and THOMAS DAVIS BELL (32), were indicted for a conspiracy.
MR. LILLEY conducted the Prosecution.
JACOB FITCHETT . I am a traveller in the employ of Messrs. Sears and Co.—the firm, I believe, consists of two persons—I knew French some years ago, but had lost sight of him several years—early in April he came into the Duke of Clarence public-house, where I was, and said that he wanted about 20 l. worth of wine—I took him over to my stores, and introduced him to my stock-manager, Mr. Vickers—some wine was tasted, and an order was given to the amount of 40 l.—he said that he was a provision—dealer, and wanted it for captains, to facilitate business—Mr. Vickers asked him for a reference, stating that the terms were cash and discount, or a bill at three months—when we left the stores I told him that it was a larger amount than I felt justified in executing, and he said that he would leave it in my hands to reduce it to the amount previously stated, 20 l.—we went to the Duke of Clarence again, and an arrangement was made that I should meet him at the Black Prince, Waterloo-road—I did so on Wednesday, and the first thing he said was, "What is the reason my order is not executed?"—I said, "You said that you could give a reference, and we have had no name give to us"—he said, "Oh, my dear fellow, I can give you forty," but he mentioned no name—he asked me bow I should be situated the following morning, and I appointed to meet him at Aldgate Church—I did so, and we proceeded to High-street, Whitechapel—he did not mention the name of any reference during the walk, but when we got within a few steps of the premises of Merry and Nutter, I saw a person of the appearance of a gentleman, standing on the steps, leaning against the doorpost, with a pen behind his ear—French said "There is Mr. Henry Nutter; wait while I speak to him"—he went up to the person, topped with him half a minute, and then turned back to me, and said, "Mr. Nutter will tell you all that you require."—I went up to the gentle-man, and said, "Mr. Nutter, I presume?"—he nodded—I repeated the question, and he said, "That is my name"—I asked him if Mr. French was trustworthy to the amount of 20 l. or 30 l.—he said, "Perfectly"—I said "He might be, in the provision line, but would he be for a luxury?"—he said, "What do you call a luxury?"—I said, "Wine"—he said, "Perfectly, for we as frequently owe him money as he does us," that he was perfectly solvent, and we need not have the slightest fear—I returned, and made a report to Mr. Vickers.
Cross-examined by MR. LANGFORD. Q. Would you have sent the 20 l. worth, without a reference? A. No—agreed to send it—I met French on the Tuesday at the Black Prince, in the Walworth-road, and he asked me why the wine was not delivered—I only know Mr. Lansdale by meeting him at the Duke of Clarence—I went to his house with the detective after the
prisoners were given in custody, and asked Mr. Lansdale to give me a copy of the invoice, but did not get it—I was not present when the bill watt given—French did not tell me to go and make the inquiries of Nutter, but I thought that was the person he intended as a reference.
MR. TILLEY. Q. Did he say, "That is Mr. Nutter, who will tell yon all that you require?" A. Yes; I sent directions from Vickers—I did not know Lonsdale when we first met at the Duke of Clarence—I met him at the Black Prince the following Wednesday week—French said, "Mr. Lansdale will become a reference as well"—Bell came to the stores in King's Arms-yard with a horse and cart, to take the things away.
MR. LANGFORD. Q. Did not you meet French again at the Black Prince when the bill was accepted? A. Yes; I said that the bill had not arrived, and be said that he had sent it by post that very day—I never made inquiries of Lonsdale—when I say that it was agreed to send the wine, I mean subject to the reference—it was a private understanding of ours that we should not deliver it without a reference, but French did not understand that.
JOHN VICKERS . I am manager of the stores of Levaux & Co. King's-Arms-yard, Tooley-street—about 8th or 10th April, French came with Fitehett—French proposed to buy some wine, and said that his object in coming to us was, that he should get something good for the particular class of trade which he had, the provision trade; as it was to give to parties with whom he was doing business, to tempt his customers to business—he mentioned captains—he gave me an order amounting to about 40 l.—I said that I could not undertake it unless he gave us satisfactory references—he said that he should be able to give most satisfactory references—on 6th January, I desired Fitehett to inquire into the character of the supposed references, and some days afterwards he made a report to me in consequence of which I allowed the goods to be delivered—it had been reduced to 23 l. or 24 l. at that time—he gave me this card ( produced) when he was introduced—Bell is the person to whom the goods were delivered on 16th April—he produced a card similar to the one French gave me, in consequence of which I gave directions to the warehouseman—I did not see the wine delivered, but two dozen only were delivered at first, as I was very busy—I was present the second time Bell came, but did not see any part of the goods delivered to him—I saw a bottle of wine at the police-office—I did not taste it, but from its peculiar structure and seal I had no doubt it was mine—after the order was executed, French came again, and we arranged to take a bill at three months, on the presumption that the references were right—the bill ( produced) was drawn in our counting-house and forwarded on the Tuesday to the address on the card which French gave, and it afterwards returned accepted—we acknowledged it, and afterwards received back from the Post-office this envelope of our letter, marked "Gone away; not known where. "
WILLIAM BACK . I am clerk to Messrs. Levaux—on 16th April, I delivered two dozen of wine by Mr. Vickers' direction to Bell, who produced a card which I gave to Mr. Vickers—he signed "J. Henderson" to the counterfoil of the delivery note in my presence—he came again on 15th April, and I delivered ten dozen, for which he signed another counterfoil "J. Henderson."
EBENEZER BUILDER . I am clerk to Merry & Nutter of Whitechapel, wholesale provision merchants in a large way of business—there is not only no Mr. Henry Nutter in the firm, but no Mr. Nutter—I have no knowledge of French—my position is such, that if he was a customer I should be aware of it.
ALFRED WATERER . I am a house-agent, of Alma Cottage, Commercial-road, Peckham—I have the letting of 16, Cottage Grove, which is near the Duke of Sussex, Commercial-road, the front of which is in Park-road—Bell
took the house at the end of February for twelve months, and entered into occupation a day or two afterwards—he told me that he belonged to the Customs—he was dressed quite like a gentleman—I found the house vacant about 23d April—I had not received any notification from Bell of his leaving—he paid me no rent—he gave the name of John French.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you quite certain it was Bell who took the house? A. I have not the least doubt of him—I was satisfied with his references—he called on me one day, and wanted to come into the house the following day, as he had got a fortnight's leave—I saw him, very respectably dressed, doing up the front garden, a fortnight after he entered into occupation, and did not see him again till he was in the police-court.
WILLIAM CHILD . I am assistant to a Sheriff's officer—for certain reasons I watched No. 16, Cottage Grove, Peckham, for about a week before 23d April—I saw Bell go in three times, and French three or four times—I also saw Lansdale go in with French several times—the house was closed on Thursday morning, the 23d at 6 o'clock.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you A. Assistant to Mr. Jackson, the Sheriff of London—I have been with him about two months before that—I was an officer at Red Lion Wharf for about sixteen months, and before that I was an officer of the City of London—I was beadle of Trinity-square twenty years—I have never been charged at the Mansion-house with perjury, or anything else—I was watching this house for the Loan Society people, Lisson Grove—I made no communication to Messrs. Levaux—I happened to be at the Court, and saw the prisoner brought up.
ROBERT MARTIN . I am landlord of the Duke of Sussex, Cumberland-road, Peckham—I saw French at the police-station, and fancied I had seen him in the house one day—Lonsdale came to my house two or three times a week—he brought me twenty-three bottles of port and sherry to sell; it was then that 1 fancy I saw French there, but I did not speak to him—I do not know that I saw Bell; but there were several in the bar, as I have an ordinary at 1 o'clock—the wine was on the kitchen table when I came in—I bought twenty-one bottles, and afterwards sold one bottle of the port to Broderick, the constable—two bottles were opened as samples, and drank by Lonsdale, myself, and two friends.
JOHN BRODERICK ( Policeman, M 210). I produce a bottle of wine which I bought of Martin—I showed it to Mr. Vickers—I took French on 1st May—he' was with Lonsdale, in Brunswick-street, Blackfriars-road—he at tempted to run away, but I overtook him—I told him the charge was conspiring to defraud Messrs. Vickers & Co. of 25 l. worth of wine, and asked if his name was French—he said, "No; I do not know anybody of that name"—I took him as far as Lonsdale's shop—he wanted to go in there, but I would not allow him—I took him to the station—the inspector asked him his name—he said, "French"—I took Bell about a quarter-past 9, on 2 d. May, in Lucretia-street, Lambet—I told him the charge, and asked him if his name was Bell—he said, "No"—he asked two or three times what the charge was; and I said, "Do you know a man named French?"—he said, "Your duty is to caution me that what I say will appear in evidence against me"—I said, "You are very right"—he begged to be treated as a gentleman, as he was a solicitor—when we got to Blackman-street, Borough, he said, "If I know French, I will eat a b—y lamp-post"—the inspector at the station asked him his name—he said, "Bell"—he was dressed in a reversible coat; he looks cleaner now than he did then.
GUILTY .*— Confined Twelve Months each.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, JUNE 8 TH, 1863.
